People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I suppose what brought me in is that I don’t feel like myself at all anymore. Since my wife’s fall, everything has sort of… come apart. I used to be the person with the lists, the calendar, the plan for everything. I’m retired now, but I still kept things organized—bills, housework, calls, even mentoring people now and then. I was the one who knew what had to be done and did it.

And now I sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I just… freeze. Sometimes I end up crying there, which is not like me. Or I snap over some tiny thing, something that doesn’t deserve it at all, and then I feel awful. Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.

It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.

I was the one who knew what had to be done and did it.\n\nAnd now I sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I just… freeze. Sometimes I end up crying there, which is not like me. Or I snap over some tiny thing, something that doesn’t deserve it at all, and then I feel awful. Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.

I suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.

Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.

So… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.

I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.

For example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.

So… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.

My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.

But I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.

I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brought me in is that I don’t feel like myself at all anymore. Since my wife’s fall, everything has sort of… come apart. I used to be the person with the lists, the calendar, the plan for everything. I’m retired now, but I still kept things organized—bills, housework, calls, even mentoring people now and then. I was the one who knew what had to be done and did it.\n\nAnd now I sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I just… freeze. Sometimes I end up crying there, which is not like me. Or I snap over some tiny thing, something that doesn’t deserve it at all, and then I feel awful. Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.

It’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.

Sometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.

This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.

It really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.

So yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.

It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.

There are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.

Even small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.

I was the one who knew what had to be done and did it.\n\nAnd now I sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I just… freeze. Sometimes I end up crying there, which is not like me. Or I snap over some tiny thing, something that doesn’t deserve it at all, and then I feel awful. Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F32.9", "F33.9", "F43.22", "F43.23"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping most days — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes or no on that one. I mean, sleep hasn’t felt especially restful lately, but when you ask it that specifically — trouble falling asleep, waking up in the night, waking too early — I have to stop and think, because I haven’t really been tracking it in that kind of organized way.

Some nights I’m aware of lying there longer than I’d like, but then I’m not sure if it’s actually a major sleep problem or just me being preoccupied and noticing the time more. And I do sometimes wake up and have that feeling of, “Oh, here I am awake again,” but I couldn’t honestly say whether that’s most days or just the nights that stand out because they’re frustrating.

So… I wouldn’t want to claim it’s been a definite pattern every night or most nights. I’ve noticed my sleep feels off at times, yes, but I’m not sure it’s consistent enough for me to say it’s one of the main things.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brought me in is that I don’t feel like myself at all anymore. Since my wife’s fall, everything has sort of… come apart. I used to be the person with the lists, the calendar, the plan for everything. I’m retired now, but I still kept things organized—bills, housework, calls, even mentoring people now and then. I was the one who knew what had to be done and did it.\n\nAnd now I sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I just… freeze. Sometimes I end up crying there, which is not like me. Or I snap over some tiny thing, something that doesn’t deserve it at all, and then I feel awful. Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve stopped showing up for mentoring and community commitments, and things I used to handle without months, since my wife’s fall changed everything at home.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S081\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping most days — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes or no on that one. I mean, sleep hasn’t felt especially restful lately, but when you ask it that specifically — trouble falling asleep, waking up in the night, waking too early — I have to stop and think, because I haven’t really been tracking it in that kind of organized way.\n\nSome nights I’m aware of lying there longer than I’d like, but then I’m not sure if it’s actually a major sleep problem or just me being preoccupied and noticing the time more. And I do sometimes wake up and have that feeling of, “Oh, here I am awake again,” but I couldn’t honestly say whether that’s most days or just the nights that stand out because they’re frustrating.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to claim it’s been a definite pattern every night or most nights. I’ve noticed my sleep feels off at times, yes, but I’m not sure it’s consistent enough for me to say it’s one of the main things.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently — like eating much less or much more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. At least, not in the way you’re describing — like a clear loss of appetite or eating much more, or clothes suddenly fitting differently.

I mean, my eating probably hasn’t been as regular as it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been tracking it closely. Some days I might not feel very interested in food, and then other days I eat more or less normally, so it’s hard for me to call that a significant change. And as far as weight, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but I haven’t noticed a big gain or loss.

So… no, I don’t think appetite or weight has been one of the main things I’ve noticed.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brought me in is that I don’t feel like myself at all anymore. Since my wife’s fall, everything has sort of… come apart. I used to be the person with the lists, the calendar, the plan for everything. I’m retired now, but I still kept things organized—bills, housework, calls, even mentoring people now and then. I was the one who knew what had to be done and did it.\n\nAnd now I sit at the kitchen table staring at her medication chart like it’s written in another language, and I just… freeze. Sometimes I end up crying there, which is not like me. Or I snap over some tiny thing, something that doesn’t deserve it at all, and then I feel awful. Meanwhile the bills are piling up, the house is getting away from me, I’m missing calls I meant to return, basic chores aren’t getting done—and it’s not that I don’t know they need doing. I know. I can see it all. I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping most days — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes or no on that one. I mean, sleep hasn’t felt especially restful lately, but when you ask it that specifically — trouble falling asleep, waking up in the night, waking too early — I have to stop and think, because I haven’t really been tracking it in that kind of organized way.\n\nSome nights I’m aware of lying there longer than I’d like, but then I’m not sure if it’s actually a major sleep problem or just me being preoccupied and noticing the time more. And I do sometimes wake up and have that feeling of, “Oh, here I am awake again,” but I couldn’t honestly say whether that’s most days or just the nights that stand out because they’re frustrating.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to claim it’s been a definite pattern every night or most nights. I’ve noticed my sleep feels off at times, yes, but I’m not sure it’s consistent enough for me to say it’s one of the main things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently — like eating much less or much more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. At least, not in the way you’re describing — like a clear loss of appetite or eating much more, or clothes suddenly fitting differently.\n\nI mean, my eating probably hasn’t been as regular as it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been tracking it closely. Some days I might not feel very interested in food, and then other days I eat more or less normally, so it’s hard for me to call that a significant change. And as far as weight, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but I haven’t noticed a big gain or loss.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think appetite or weight has been one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not really sure I’ve noticed that, no. Not in a way that feels obvious to me.

I mean, I suppose when I’m having a harder day I may move a little more slowly just because I’m less eager to get going, but I don’t know that anyone would look at me and say I’m noticeably slowed down. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve.
