But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping most days — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes or no on that one. I mean, sleep hasn’t felt especially restful lately, but when you ask it that specifically — trouble falling asleep, waking up in the night, waking too early — I have to stop and think, because I haven’t really been tracking it in that kind of organized way.\n\nSome nights I’m aware of lying there longer than I’d like, but then I’m not sure if it’s actually a major sleep problem or just me being preoccupied and noticing the time more. And I do sometimes wake up and have that feeling of, “Oh, here I am awake again,” but I couldn’t honestly say whether that’s most days or just the nights that stand out because they’re frustrating.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to claim it’s been a definite pattern every night or most nights. I’ve noticed my sleep feels off at times, yes, but I’m not sure it’s consistent enough for me to say it’s one of the main things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently — like eating much less or much more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. At least, not in the way you’re describing — like a clear loss of appetite or eating much more, or clothes suddenly fitting differently.\n\nI mean, my eating probably hasn’t been as regular as it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been tracking it closely. Some days I might not feel very interested in food, and then other days I eat more or less normally, so it’s hard for me to call that a significant change. And as far as weight, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but I haven’t noticed a big gain or loss.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think appetite or weight has been one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless, more than you normally would be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’ve noticed that, no. Not in a way that feels obvious to me.\n\nI mean, I suppose when I’m having a harder day I may move a little more slowly just because I’m less eager to get going, but I don’t know that anyone would look at me and say I’m noticeably slowed down. And I haven’t had that feeling of being unable to sit still, pacing around the room, or fidgeting constantly — at least not more than what I’d consider normal for me.\n\nIt’s funny, because when you ask it, I start reviewing little things in my head — how fast I walk, whether I’m getting out of chairs more slowly, whether I’m tapping my fingers or shifting around — but I can’t say there’s a clear change there. If anything, it’s more that I feel slowed down inside sometimes, but physically moving slower or being unusually restless… I don’t think that’s been a major thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there have been days when nothing — not even things you usually enjoy — felt pleasurable or rewarding at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly. I mean, there are definitely days when things feel kind of flat, or I don’t get as much out of them as I used to, but “nothing felt pleasurable at all” is a pretty absolute way to say it.\n\nIf I’m thinking honestly, I can still have little moments where something is pleasant — a cup of coffee, a familiar show, a phone call that goes better than expected, that sort of thing. It may not feel as rich or satisfying as I’d like, and sometimes I almost have to remind myself that I’m supposed to enjoy it, which is a strange feeling to describe. But I don’t think I can say there are days when absolutely nothing feels even a little rewarding.\n\nSo… no, not really in that complete sense. More like enjoyment has been muted at times, rather than entirely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's been harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, more than you'd expect from just being tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I could say that’s been a clear problem, at least not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have moments where I’m a little scattered, but I tend to assume that’s just… life, or being preoccupied, or not sleeping perfectly, or having too many things on my mind.\n\nWhen it comes to thinking clearly, I can still follow conversations, read something if I sit down with it, pay bills, make ordinary decisions. I may take a bit longer sometimes because I go back and forth in my head, but I’m not sure that’s really a change in concentration so much as me overthinking things. And with decisions, I’ve never been someone who just snaps into an answer immediately, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s new from what’s just my usual way.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… not really. I haven’t noticed a major decline in concentration or decision-making that feels beyond normal tiredness or stress.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you've had a hard time getting out of bed or starting your day, even beyond just feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though maybe not always in the literal sense of “I can’t get out of bed.” It’s more that starting the day has become this whole ordeal. I’ll be awake, and I know what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to move into it the way I used to.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting things pretty badly. I’ve stopped showing up for mentoring things I used to be very reliable about, and some community commitments too. Those weren’t just optional hobbies for me — they were part of how I structured my week, part of how I felt useful. Now I’ll let them slide, or I’ll avoid responding, and then the longer I leave it, the harder it is to re-enter.\n\nAt home, too, it’s embarrassing to say, but ordinary things are slipping. Bills that I would have handled without even thinking, little upkeep around the house, keeping basic routines going — laundry, mail, tidying, that sort of thing — they’re not getting done the way they should. It’s not that I don’t understand what needs to happen. I do. I can list it all in my head. But getting from knowing it to actually starting… that’s where I get stuck.\n\nSo yes, most days there’s a real difficulty getting going. It feels like the day is already too much before I’ve even properly begun.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so overwhelmed or hopeless that you've thought about not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve definitely felt overwhelmed, yes. And there have been moments where I’ve thought, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” or “I wish I could just not have to deal with any of it for a while.” But when you say “not wanting to live anymore,” that sounds very serious, and I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to disappear from the pressure of it, if that makes sense. Not so much a clear thought of ending my life. I haven’t sat there thinking through something like that. But I have had that exhausted, hopeless feeling where life feels like too much in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’ve had thoughts of wanting relief, wanting to not be in the middle of all this, but I don’t know that I’ve truly thought, “I don’t want to be alive.”\n\nDoctor: Before this current period following your wife’s fall, have you ever had a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a clear period like that, no. I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, of course — I don’t think anyone gets to my age without some low periods or times where you’re less interested in things. But if you’re asking whether there was a distinct episode before all of this with my wife, where for two weeks or more I was really down or couldn’t enjoy things in a way that stood out as unusual… I don’t know that I’d say yes.\n\nI’ve always had times where I get serious, or withdrawn for a bit, especially when there’s a lot going on, but it didn’t feel like this kind of prolonged thing I could mark on a calendar. So I’d say not that I clearly remember. Nothing that I would have identified at the time as a major spell of being down or losing interest for weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I keep coming back to the timing, because that’s what makes it feel different to me. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and before that, I don’t think I was in some long decline or anything like that. I had ordinary ups and downs, but I was still functioning like myself.

It was after the fall — after the caregiving routine sort of took over everything — that I really started falling apart. At first I told myself I was just adjusting, that anyone would feel strained, but the whole day became organized around her needs and appointments and watching for what might go wrong. And somewhere in that, I stopped feeling like I could keep up.

So if you’re asking whether there were several-day stretches before her fall where I felt unusually down or uninterested, I don’t think that’s really where this began. Not in any way that stands out to me. The change feels tied to what happened afterward, when caregiving became the center of our lives.

I just can’t seem to make myself function the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and were you experiencing similar difficulties before your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months, I think. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and it wasn’t like this before that. I mean, I had normal worries, normal older-person frustrations, I suppose, but I wasn’t falling apart the way I am now.\n\nIt really started after the caregiving routine took over—once everything became centered on what she needed, what had to be done next, keeping track of things, making sure I didn’t miss something important. That’s when I began to feel like I couldn’t keep up and like I wasn’t myself. Before the fall, I was managing. I had my routines. I could handle our life. This came after.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. I mean… I haven’t really had the room to notice whether I “enjoy” things, if that makes sense. Mentoring, for example, it’s not that I suddenly decided I hate it or don’t care about it anymore. It’s more that my mind is so taken up with everything at home that I don’t get to it, or I put it off, and then I feel bad about putting it off.\n\nI suppose when something used to feel satisfying, now it feels… complicated. Like there’s a weight attached to it, because I’m distracted or I’m thinking I should be doing something else. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest in it exactly. I still think those things matter. I just haven’t been able to settle into them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or sad for much of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s “even when nothing specific is going wrong,” because in my mind there’s almost always something specific hovering there, if that makes sense. I’ll feel low, yes, or get tearful, but it’s usually tied to what’s happening at home or to the thought that I’m not keeping up the way I should.\n\nSo… most days I’m not exactly cheerful, no. But I’m not sure I wake up sad for no reason. It’s more that something reminds me—some task, some responsibility, some thing I’ve let slide—and then the sadness sort of comes in around that. I may be splitting hairs, I don’t know. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad all day out of nowhere, because it feels more connected to the situation than that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or satisfaction in something, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one… and I don’t want to say no too quickly, because I suppose there are little moments. Not “enjoyment” in the way I used to mean it, where I’d feel relaxed and completely taken up by something, but maybe a small bit of satisfaction here and there.\n\nFor example, if I manage to get through something I was supposed to do without making a mess of it, there’s a brief feeling of relief. I don’t know if that counts as enjoyment. It’s more like, “All right, that didn’t go badly.” And sometimes if I have a short conversation with someone and it goes smoothly, I can feel almost normal for a few minutes. But then my mind tends to go right back to everything else.\n\nSo… genuine enjoyment? I’m not sure. Short bits of relief, maybe. Small moments where I’m not completely tangled up in it. But I can’t think of a recent time where I felt really absorbed and pleased the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re failing at things more than you reasonably should, even when you’re doing your best?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I do feel guilty, yes, but whether it’s “more than I reasonably should” is the part I get stuck on. Because in the moment it feels reasonable to me. If something doesn’t get done, or I handle something poorly, my first thought is that I should have managed it better. And then I can go over it in my head a lot.\n\nBut I also know, if I were talking to someone else in the same position, I probably wouldn’t be as harsh with them as I am with myself. I can see that intellectually. It’s just hard to feel it. I’ll tell myself, “Well, you’re doing what you can,” and then another part of me says, “Yes, but it still wasn’t enough.” So I suppose there is a sense of failing, or falling short, even when I’m trying.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly every time. Sometimes it’s more like shame, or disappointment in myself, or this constant feeling that I should be more capable than I am. At my age, I feel like I should have a steadier handle on things. So… yes, I think that’s been there, but I have trouble judging whether it’s excessive or just me seeing the situation clearly.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a day or two, not just that they feel more difficult or less satisfying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “completely lost,” no. Or at least… I’d hesitate to say that, because it sounds very absolute, like I couldn’t feel anything at all from things I used to like, and I don’t know that that’s quite right.\n\nIt’s more that the things feel harder to start, and when I do them they don’t give me as much as they used to. I might sit down with something I normally would have enjoyed and find myself sort of going through the motions, or not staying with it very long. But I can’t honestly say there was a clear day or two where everything I used to enjoy gave me no pleasure whatsoever. It’s murkier than that.\n\nSometimes I avoid things because I don’t feel up to them, or because I assume I won’t enjoy them, and then that probably makes it look like I’ve lost interest. But if you’re asking whether the interest or pleasure is completely gone, I don’t know. I think it’s dulled. Muted, maybe. Not absent in a clean, definite way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there was a time in the past two months when you felt down or unmotivated nearly every day for at least two weeks in a row, even if you were managing some things during that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I would say that. It’s a little hard to separate one week from another, because it’s all blurred together, but if I look back honestly, this has been going on for about two months now.\n\nIt really seemed to start after my wife’s fall, when everything at home changed. Before that I had normal ups and downs, of course, but this has felt different — more constant. Since then, I’d say I’ve felt down or unmotivated most days, probably nearly every day, even though I’ve still had to keep doing certain things. It’s not as if I stopped functioning completely, but the feeling has been there in the background almost all the time.\n\nSo yes, at least two weeks in a row… more than that, really. It’s been about two months since that happened, and I haven’t quite felt like myself since.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling fatigued or have been lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that very cleanly. I do feel tired a lot, but I don’t know how much of that is actual fatigue versus just feeling worn down mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are days when I haven’t done much at all and I still feel like I don’t have much in reserve. But then I start questioning myself — well, maybe I didn’t sleep well, or maybe I’ve been tense, or maybe I’m just getting older. I don’t want to overstate it and say, yes, absolutely, I’m exhausted most days for no reason. It’s more… I notice I don’t have the same push I normally would.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require more effort to get started. But I wouldn’t say I’m physically collapsing or anything like that. It’s not that dramatic. It’s more like I’m dragging, or I have to talk myself into doing things that should be fairly ordinary. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping most days — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes or no on that one. I mean, sleep hasn’t felt especially restful lately, but when you ask it that specifically — trouble falling asleep, waking up in the night, waking too early — I have to stop and think, because I haven’t really been tracking it in that kind of organized way.\n\nSome nights I’m aware of lying there longer than I’d like, but then I’m not sure if it’s actually a major sleep problem or just me being preoccupied and noticing the time more. And I do sometimes wake up and have that feeling of, “Oh, here I am awake again,” but I couldn’t honestly say whether that’s most days or just the nights that stand out because they’re frustrating.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to claim it’s been a definite pattern every night or most nights. I’ve noticed my sleep feels off at times, yes, but I’m not sure it’s consistent enough for me to say it’s one of the main things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently — like eating much less or much more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. At least, not in the way you’re describing — like a clear loss of appetite or eating much more, or clothes suddenly fitting differently.\n\nI mean, my eating probably hasn’t been as regular as it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been tracking it closely. Some days I might not feel very interested in food, and then other days I eat more or less normally, so it’s hard for me to call that a significant change. And as far as weight, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but I haven’t noticed a big gain or loss.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think appetite or weight has been one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless, more than you normally would be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’ve noticed that, no. Not in a way that feels obvious to me.\n\nI mean, I suppose when I’m having a harder day I may move a little more slowly just because I’m less eager to get going, but I don’t know that anyone would look at me and say I’m noticeably slowed down. And I haven’t had that feeling of being unable to sit still, pacing around the room, or fidgeting constantly — at least not more than what I’d consider normal for me.\n\nIt’s funny, because when you ask it, I start reviewing little things in my head — how fast I walk, whether I’m getting out of chairs more slowly, whether I’m tapping my fingers or shifting around — but I can’t say there’s a clear change there. If anything, it’s more that I feel slowed down inside sometimes, but physically moving slower or being unusually restless… I don’t think that’s been a major thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there have been days when nothing — not even things you usually enjoy — felt pleasurable or rewarding at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly. I mean, there are definitely days when things feel kind of flat, or I don’t get as much out of them as I used to, but “nothing felt pleasurable at all” is a pretty absolute way to say it.\n\nIf I’m thinking honestly, I can still have little moments where something is pleasant — a cup of coffee, a familiar show, a phone call that goes better than expected, that sort of thing. It may not feel as rich or satisfying as I’d like, and sometimes I almost have to remind myself that I’m supposed to enjoy it, which is a strange feeling to describe. But I don’t think I can say there are days when absolutely nothing feels even a little rewarding.\n\nSo… no, not really in that complete sense. More like enjoyment has been muted at times, rather than entirely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's been harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, more than you'd expect from just being tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I could say that’s been a clear problem, at least not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have moments where I’m a little scattered, but I tend to assume that’s just… life, or being preoccupied, or not sleeping perfectly, or having too many things on my mind.\n\nWhen it comes to thinking clearly, I can still follow conversations, read something if I sit down with it, pay bills, make ordinary decisions. I may take a bit longer sometimes because I go back and forth in my head, but I’m not sure that’s really a change in concentration so much as me overthinking things. And with decisions, I’ve never been someone who just snaps into an answer immediately, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s new from what’s just my usual way.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… not really. I haven’t noticed a major decline in concentration or decision-making that feels beyond normal tiredness or stress.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you've had a hard time getting out of bed or starting your day, even beyond just feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though maybe not always in the literal sense of “I can’t get out of bed.” It’s more that starting the day has become this whole ordeal. I’ll be awake, and I know what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to move into it the way I used to.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting things pretty badly. I’ve stopped showing up for mentoring things I used to be very reliable about, and some community commitments too. Those weren’t just optional hobbies for me — they were part of how I structured my week, part of how I felt useful. Now I’ll let them slide, or I’ll avoid responding, and then the longer I leave it, the harder it is to re-enter.\n\nAt home, too, it’s embarrassing to say, but ordinary things are slipping. Bills that I would have handled without even thinking, little upkeep around the house, keeping basic routines going — laundry, mail, tidying, that sort of thing — they’re not getting done the way they should. It’s not that I don’t understand what needs to happen. I do. I can list it all in my head. But getting from knowing it to actually starting… that’s where I get stuck.\n\nSo yes, most days there’s a real difficulty getting going. It feels like the day is already too much before I’ve even properly begun.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so overwhelmed or hopeless that you've thought about not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve definitely felt overwhelmed, yes. And there have been moments where I’ve thought, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” or “I wish I could just not have to deal with any of it for a while.” But when you say “not wanting to live anymore,” that sounds very serious, and I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to disappear from the pressure of it, if that makes sense. Not so much a clear thought of ending my life. I haven’t sat there thinking through something like that. But I have had that exhausted, hopeless feeling where life feels like too much in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’ve had thoughts of wanting relief, wanting to not be in the middle of all this, but I don’t know that I’ve truly thought, “I don’t want to be alive.”\n\nDoctor: Before this current period following your wife’s fall, have you ever had a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a clear period like that, no. I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, of course — I don’t think anyone gets to my age without some low periods or times where you’re less interested in things. But if you’re asking whether there was a distinct episode before all of this with my wife, where for two weeks or more I was really down or couldn’t enjoy things in a way that stood out as unusual… I don’t know that I’d say yes.\n\nI’ve always had times where I get serious, or withdrawn for a bit, especially when there’s a lot going on, but it didn’t feel like this kind of prolonged thing I could mark on a calendar. So I’d say not that I clearly remember. So… yes, I’ve noticed low energy, but I’m not sure how to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping most days — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you’d like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes or no on that one. And I do sometimes wake up and have that feeling of, “Oh, here I am awake again,” but I couldn’t honestly say whether that’s most days or just the nights that stand out because they’re frustrating.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t want to claim it’s been a definite pattern every night or most nights. I’ve noticed my sleep feels off at times, yes, but I’m not sure it’s consistent enough for me to say it’s one of the main things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently — like eating much less or much more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. At least, not in the way you’re describing — like a clear loss of appetite or eating much more, or clothes suddenly fitting differently.\n\nI mean, my eating probably hasn’t been as regular as it used to be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been tracking it closely. Some days I might not feel very interested in food, and then other days I eat more or less normally, so it’s hard for me to call that a significant change. And as far as weight, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully, but I haven’t noticed a big gain or loss.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think appetite or weight has been one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or maybe fidgety and restless, more than you normally would be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’ve noticed that, no. Not in a way that feels obvious to me.\n\nI mean, I suppose when I’m having a harder day I may move a little more slowly just because I’m less eager to get going, but I don’t know that anyone would look at me and say I’m noticeably slowed down. And I haven’t had that feeling of being unable to sit still, pacing around the room, or fidgeting constantly — at least not more than what I’d consider normal for me.\n\nIt’s funny, because when you ask it, I start reviewing little things in my head — how fast I walk, whether I’m getting out of chairs more slowly, whether I’m tapping my fingers or shifting around — but I can’t say there’s a clear change there. If anything, it’s more that I feel slowed down inside sometimes, but physically moving slower or being unusually restless… I don’t think that’s been a major thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there have been days when nothing — not even things you usually enjoy — felt pleasurable or rewarding at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly. I mean, there are definitely days when things feel kind of flat, or I don’t get as much out of them as I used to, but “nothing felt pleasurable at all” is a pretty absolute way to say it.\n\nIf I’m thinking honestly, I can still have little moments where something is pleasant — a cup of coffee, a familiar show, a phone call that goes better than expected, that sort of thing. It may not feel as rich or satisfying as I’d like, and sometimes I almost have to remind myself that I’m supposed to enjoy it, which is a strange feeling to describe. But I don’t think I can say there are days when absolutely nothing feels even a little rewarding.\n\nSo… no, not really in that complete sense. More like enjoyment has been muted at times, rather than entirely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's been harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, more than you'd expect from just being tired or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I could say that’s been a clear problem, at least not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have moments where I’m a little scattered, but I tend to assume that’s just… life, or being preoccupied, or not sleeping perfectly, or having too many things on my mind.\n\nWhen it comes to thinking clearly, I can still follow conversations, read something if I sit down with it, pay bills, make ordinary decisions. I may take a bit longer sometimes because I go back and forth in my head, but I’m not sure that’s really a change in concentration so much as me overthinking things. And with decisions, I’ve never been someone who just snaps into an answer immediately, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s new from what’s just my usual way.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… not really. I haven’t noticed a major decline in concentration or decision-making that feels beyond normal tiredness or stress.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you've had a hard time getting out of bed or starting your day, even beyond just feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though maybe not always in the literal sense of “I can’t get out of bed.” It’s more that starting the day has become this whole ordeal. I’ll be awake, and I know what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to move into it the way I used to.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting things pretty badly. I’ve stopped showing up for mentoring things I used to be very reliable about, and some community commitments too. Those weren’t just optional hobbies for me — they were part of how I structured my week, part of how I felt useful. Now I’ll let them slide, or I’ll avoid responding, and then the longer I leave it, the harder it is to re-enter.\n\nAt home, too, it’s embarrassing to say, but ordinary things are slipping. Bills that I would have handled without even thinking, little upkeep around the house, keeping basic routines going — laundry, mail, tidying, that sort of thing — they’re not getting done the way they should. It’s not that I don’t understand what needs to happen. I do. I can list it all in my head. But getting from knowing it to actually starting… that’s where I get stuck.\n\nSo yes, most days there’s a real difficulty getting going. It feels like the day is already too much before I’ve even properly begun.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so overwhelmed or hopeless that you've thought about not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I’ve definitely felt overwhelmed, yes. And there have been moments where I’ve thought, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” or “I wish I could just not have to deal with any of it for a while.” But when you say “not wanting to live anymore,” that sounds very serious, and I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to disappear from the pressure of it, if that makes sense. Not so much a clear thought of ending my life. I haven’t sat there thinking through something like that. But I have had that exhausted, hopeless feeling where life feels like too much in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’ve had thoughts of wanting relief, wanting to not be in the middle of all this, but I don’t know that I’ve truly thought, “I don’t want to be alive.”\n\nDoctor: Before this current period following your wife’s fall, have you ever had a time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a clear period like that, no. I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, of course — I don’t think anyone gets to my age without some low periods or times where you’re less interested in things. But if you’re asking whether there was a distinct episode before all of this with my wife, where for two weeks or more I was really down or couldn’t enjoy things in a way that stood out as unusual… I don’t know that I’d say yes.\n\nI’ve always had times where I get serious, or withdrawn for a bit, especially when there’s a lot going on, but it didn’t feel like this kind of prolonged thing I could mark on a calendar. So I’d say not that I clearly remember. Nothing that I would have identified at the time as a major spell of being down or losing interest for weeks.\n\nDoctor: Looking back further, even if not as clearly defined as two weeks, have you ever had a period in your life before your wife’s fall where you felt down or lost interest in things for several days or more in a way that stood out from your usual mood?\n\nPatient: I keep coming back to the timing, because that’s what makes it feel different to me. My wife’s fall was only about two months ago, and before that, I don’t think I was in some long decline or anything like that. I had ordinary ups and downs, but I was still functioning like myself.\n\nIt was after the fall — after the caregiving routine sort of took over everything — that I really started falling apart. At first I told myself I was just adjusting, that anyone would feel strained, but the whole day became organized around her needs and appointments and watching for what might go wrong. And somewhere in that, I stopped feeling like I could keep up.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there were several-day stretches before her fall where I felt unusually down or uninterested, I don’t think that’s really where this began. Not in any way that stands out to me. The change feels tied to what happened afterward, when caregiving became the center of our lives."
